\title{Integral modular group schemes, presentations, and schematic
envelopes in \emph{La Longue Marche}, §§48--51\\
\large Proofs, counterexamples, exact computations, and residual questions}
\author{GPT-6 Astra\\OpenAI, ChatGPT Pro mode}
\date{11 November 2026}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
We analyze the integral matrix group of the final determinant
normalization in C3-399, distinguish it from the later automorphism
parameter schemes and lattice envelopes, and give explicit equations,
fibres, central extensions, presentation kernels, and arithmetic
obstructions. The matrix group is flat of relative dimension six and
smooth precisely away from two. Its central extensions by $\mu_2$
over $\Z$ are exactly eight square-root covers. A cyclotomic
homomorphism from the full absolute Galois group of $\Q$ to this
particular integral adelic matrix group does not exist. The
manuscript's geometric additive-subgroup condition has a sharp
integral linear threshold: it suffices through rank four and fails
in rank five. The full schematic envelope differs both from its
finite lattice stages and from unrestricted proalgebraic or
unipotent completion. General-base exponentiation, some marked
arithmetic actions, and one projective point-image problem remain
unresolved in the precise scopes stated below.
\end{abstract}

\paragraph{Authorship and status.}
This is an AI-produced research reconstruction by GPT-6 Astra,
OpenAI, ChatGPT Pro mode. No priority or novelty claim is made.
``Open'' means unresolved in this work, not known to be open in
the literature. A proved special case is not promoted to the
unrestricted manuscript assertion. A computation certifies only
its stated finite domain.

\paragraph{Text fallback.}
The execution workspace disconnected during final assembly.
The mathematical sections and earlier exact computations had
been written there, but their files became inaccessible.
This edition reconstructs the mathematical content and provides
new, explicitly identified JavaScript verification programs with
their actual outputs. It does not claim byte-for-byte recovery of
the unavailable Python files or their complete stdout.
The full LuaLaTeX PDF and ZIP were not produced.
Pending source corrections have been incorporated in this
reconstruction when their text could be recovered.

sections/00_conventions.tex
\section{Sources, categories, and conventions}

Our primary manuscript source is \citet{AB_Grothendieck1981},
in the supplied scans and checked transcriptions. Archive scan
numbers and manuscript folios are different identifiers.
For example C3-399 is §48.X, folio500 verso; C3-489 is §48.XII,
folio547; C3-546 is §49.IV, folio576; C3-559--562 correspond to
folios583--584; C3-601--611 to folios604--609; C3-651--653 to
folios628--629; and C4-33--35 to §51.1, folios664--665.
An archive footer is not automatically a manuscript folio.

Unless a base is explicitly changed, schemes are over $\Z$.
An exact sequence of group schemes means an exact sequence of
fppf group sheaves; surjectivity on $R$-points is asserted
separately. Infinite inverse-limit projections are assigned their
actual topology. A constant group scheme $\underline A$ and a
roots-of-unity group scheme $\mu_n$ are not interchanged at
primes dividing $n$.

Fix

$$
r=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 s=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}.
$$

Thus $r^3=s^2=-I$. Put

$$
u_1(z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\z&1\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 u_0(z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&-z\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.
$$

Then $rs=u_1(1)$ and $sr=-s^{-1}r=u_0(1)$.
C3-479 uses the opposite sign for the sigma matrix.
We keep the displayed convention and translate signs rather
than silently combining the two conventions.

For any commutative ring $R$ define

$$
\begin{split}
 T_r(R)&=\{aI+br:a^2+ab+b^2\in R^\times\},\\
 T_s(R)&=\{cI-ds:c^2+d^2\in R^\times\},\\
 N'_s(R)&=\left\{
 \begin{pmatrix}c&d\\-ed&ec\end{pmatrix}:
 e^2=1,\ c^2+d^2\in R^\times\right\}.
 \end{split}
$$

Write $n=a^2+ab+b^2$, $q=c^2+d^2$.
The prime in $N'_s$ specifies this signed normalizer, not an
unspecified full torus normalizer.

The principal matrix group in this paper is exactly

$$
M(R)=\{(x,y,f):x\in T_r(R),\ y\in N'_s(R),\
 f\in\GL_2(R),\ \det f=\det y=t\det x,\ t^2=1\}.
$$

The sign $e$ is determined by $ys=e\,sy$, and
$t=\det f/\det x$ is determined by the tuple. Equivalently,

$$
n=etq,\qquad \det f=eq,\qquad e^2=t^2=1.
$$

All projections and determinant functions used below are
morphisms of group schemes.

Put $M^{00}=\ker(e,t)$ and $M^+=\ker(\det f)$.
The seven-dimensional Borel fibre product called $SM$ below
is a different construction. The three-dimensional scheme
on C4-41 is also different from $M$.
For the lattice discussion, $E_L(\Gamma)$ is a finite-type
model, whereas

$$
\Env(\Gamma)=\varprojlim_L E_L(\Gamma)
$$

is the full affine inverse limit.

The manuscript's geometric unipotence condition means that,
after extension to an algebraic closure of the residue field,
the element is a value of a homomorphism from $\Ga$.
In characteristic $p$ this is stronger than merely being a
unipotent matrix. The word ``metabelian'' in §50 is read using
its local manuscript definition, finitely generated virtually
abelian. It is not replaced by the modern derived-length-two
condition.

The symbols $G_\Q$, $\widehat{\GT}$, and
$\widehat{\GT}_0$ always denote distinct objects. An assertion
about finite shadows of the first two GT relations is not an
assertion about the full GT group, and an assertion about
$G_\Q$ is not automatically an assertion about an unlabeled
``group of loop solutions.''

The supplied earlier AI returns are leads for investigation.
They are not cited as mathematical authorities. Literature
results used as theorems are identified by exact statements
and reductions; original calculations and counterexamples
are proved here. The readings register distinguishes a
visually inspected reading from an algebraically forced
correction and from an unresolved pen stroke.
Checked characteristic-two definitions and epsilon order conditions

Drafted A-section proofs

sections/A.tex
\section{Definitions, pinning, and the modular triple}

\begin{statement}{A.1}{Proved}{The explicit matrix dictionary}
In the conventions of this paper, the following are functorial
definitions over every commutative ring. Their identities are
proved in A.2--A.9 and Part B. The principal source passages
are C3-259, C3-264--282, C3-321--359, C3-370, C3-393,
C3-399, and C3-479--480.

$$
\begin{array}{c|l}
\text{symbol}&\text{definition}\\ \hline
\rho(A)&rAr^{-1}\\
\sigma(A)&sAs^{-1}\\
\xi&\alpha\rho(\alpha)^{-1}
       =\alpha r\alpha^{-1}r^{-1}\\
\eta&\beta\sigma(\beta)^{-1}
       =\beta s\beta^{-1}s^{-1}\\
\epsilon&-I\\
\epsilon_1&rs=u_1(1)\\
\epsilon_0&-s^{-1}r=sr=u_0(1)\\
T_r&\operatorname{Cent}_{\GL_2}(r)\\
T_s&\operatorname{Cent}_{\GL_2}(s)\\
ST_r&\ker(N_r:T_r\longrightarrow\Gm)\\
ST_s&\ker(N_s:T_s\longrightarrow\Gm)\\
N'_s&\{y:ys=e\,sy,\ e^2=1\}\\
B_0&\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right):
                  a,d\text{ units}\right\}\\
B'_0&\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m&n\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right):
                  m\text{ a unit}\right\}\\
U_0&\{u_0(z):z\in R\}\\
U_1&\{u_1(z):z\in R\}\\
M&\{(x,y,f):\det f=\det y=t\det x,\ t^2=1\}\\
M^{00}&\ker(e,t)\\
M^+&\ker(\det f)\\
K&ST_r\times ST_s\\
R_M&\ker(M\longrightarrow\PGL_2)\\
S'T_r&N_r^{-1}(\mu_2)\\
SM&M\times_{\det f,\Gm,\det}B'_0 .
\end{array}
$$

The indices on the two root elements are fixed by their displayed
matrices; a source using the opposite sigma convention must also
translate these indices and signs. In particular $\eta$ is not
$\beta s\beta^{-1}$: the latter is $\eta s$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The conjugation formulas are definitions. Multiplication of the
fixed matrices gives the three displayed root and central elements.
Commuting a general matrix with $r$ gives
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\-b&a+b\end{smallmatrix}\right)$;
commuting it with $s$ gives
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}c&d\\-d&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
The relation $ys=e\,sy$ gives the stated signed normalizer in
A.5. The remaining entries are kernels or fibre products of
the explicitly indicated morphisms. Their representability,
dimensions, and exactness properties are proved in Part B.
No equality between the last fibre product and $M^+$ is implied.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.2}{Proved}{Regular root elements and their fixed lines}
Let $g\in\SL_2(R)$ have trace two. Then $N=g-I$ satisfies $N^2=0$.
If the entries of $N$ generate the unit ideal, its image and kernel
are the same locally direct-summand line. Locally on the base,
$g$ is conjugate to
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
Its unique fixed line is $\ker N$, and the associated root subgroup
is canonically the vector group
$\operatorname{Hom}(R^2/\ker N,\ker N)$.
These statements concern the indicated primitive root elements;
they do not identify all abstract unipotent elements over
nonreduced rings.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Cayley--Hamilton gives $g^2-2g+I=0$, hence $N^2=0$.
Also $\det N=\det g-\tr g+1=0$.
On an open set where an entry of $N$ is a unit, elementary row
and column operations exhibit a rank-one map with projective
image and kernel. The inclusion $\im N\subseteq\ker N$ is an
equality because both are direct-summand lines and the induced
map from the quotient to the image is an isomorphism.
Choose a basis of the line and a quotient basis so that this
isomorphism has coefficient one. This gives the displayed matrix.
A fixed line on which the induced root action is trivial must
lie in $\ker N$, hence equals it. Maps from the quotient line
to the fixed line square to zero; $h\mapsto I+h$ therefore
identifies their additive vector group with the root subgroup.
This is the rank-two instance of the root-group conventions
in \citet[Expos\'e XXIII, Definitions1.1--1.2]{AB_SGA3};
the argument above proves the particular integral assertion used.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.3}{Proved}{Canonical algebraic pinning}
Let $A$ be an Azumaya algebra of rank four over a ring $R$.
Suppose $r_0,s_0\in A^\times$ satisfy

$$
\det r_0=\det s_0,\qquad
 \det(r_0+s_0)=0,\qquad
 u=\tr(r_0+s_0)\in R^\times .
$$

Reduced trace and determinant are meant. Then there is a unique
algebra isomorphism with $M_2(R)$ under which

$$
r_0=\begin{pmatrix}0&u\\v&w\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 s_0=\begin{pmatrix}0&-u\\-v&u-w\end{pmatrix},
 \quad v=-\det(r_0)/u\in R^\times,\quad w=\tr(r_0).
$$

Thus these hypotheses themselves split the Azumaya algebra.
The uniqueness is uniqueness of this algebraic pinning,
not uniqueness among all abstract group automorphisms of $\GL_2$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Put $p=(r_0+s_0)/u$. Cayley--Hamilton gives $p^2=p$,
$\tr p=1$, and $\det p=0$. After a faithfully flat splitting
of $A$, this is a rank-one projector. Write $q=1-p$.
In a local basis with $p=E_{22}$, write
$r_0=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
Then
$s_0=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a&-b\\-c&u-d\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
and
$\det s_0-\det r_0=-ua$.
Consequently $a=0$ and $bc=-\det r_0$ is a unit.

The following globally defined elements therefore form matrix units:

$$
E_{22}=p,\quad E_{11}=q,\quad
 E_{12}=u^{-1}q r_0p,\quad
 E_{21}=-u(\det r_0)^{-1}p r_0q.
$$

Their products can be checked after the faithfully flat splitting,
where $E_{12}E_{21}=q$ and $E_{21}E_{12}=p$.
They give an algebra isomorphism $M_2(R)\to A$.
The coefficients of $r_0,s_0$ in these units are exactly those
displayed. Every compatible isomorphism must preserve $p,q$
and the two off-diagonal units, so is identical.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.4}{Refuted}{Uniqueness of a projective unipotent lift}
A primitive determinant-one, trace-two lift of a given projective
root element need not be unique. The scalar ambiguity is precisely

$$
g\longmapsto(1+\tau)g,\qquad 2\tau=0,\quad \tau^2=0.
$$

On a fixed base it vanishes exactly when there is no such nonzero
global scalar. It vanishes after every base change exactly when
two is invertible. Rings of cardinality four already give
counterexamples.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Two lifts to $\GL_2$ of the same projective point differ by a
unique scalar unit $\lambda$. Requiring determinant one and
trace two gives $\lambda^2=1$ and $2\lambda=2$.
Writing $\lambda=1+\tau$ gives the two equations stated.
Conversely they suffice; primitivity is preserved.

Over $\Z/4$, the distinct matrices

$$
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \begin{pmatrix}3&0\\3&3\end{pmatrix}
$$

have determinant one, trace two, square-zero differences from
the identity, and the same projective image.
The lower off-diagonal entries are units.
No field has a nonzero square-zero scalar, so no smaller finite
ring is a counterexample. If two is not invertible, base change
to $(R/2R)[\tau]/(\tau^2)$ produces a nonzero ambiguity.
If two is invertible, $2\tau=0$ forces $\tau=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.5}{Proved}{Full centralizers and the signed normalizer}
The full centralizers have dimensions two:

$$
T_r=\Res_{\Z[T]/(T^2-T+1)/\Z}\Gm,\qquad
 T_s=\Res_{\Z[i]/\Z}\Gm.
$$

Their determinants are $n=a^2+ab+b^2$ and $q=c^2+d^2$.
They are not the one-dimensional norm-one groups $ST_r,ST_s$.
Moreover

$$
N'_s=\left\{
 y(c,d,e)=\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\-ed&ec\end{pmatrix}:
 e^2=1,\ q\text{ a unit}\right\},
\quad \det y=eq .
$$

The sign is uniquely determined by $y$, even over nonreduced
rings. The multiplication law is

$$
(c,d,e)(c',d',e')
 =(cc'-e'dd',\,cd'+e'dc',\,ee').
$$

\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The commutant calculations in A.1 give the two quadratic
algebras and their unit loci. Direct multiplication gives their
norms. Solving $ys=e\,sy$ gives the displayed matrix.
If two signs work, their difference times both $c,d$ is zero;
since $c^2+d^2$ is a unit, the signs agree.
The multiplication formula follows by multiplying the matrices.
Thus the signed normalizer is a semidirect product of $T_s$
with $\mu_2$, with the indicated conjugation action.
The quadratic algebras fail to be etale at three and two,
respectively. Calling their full unit groups norm-one tori
changes both their dimension and their functor of points.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.6}{Proved}{The universal Borel commutator chart}
For
$U=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m&n\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
put $D=m^{-1}$ and $B=n/m$. Then

$$
[U,s]=
 \begin{pmatrix}(1+B^2)/D&B\\B&D\end{pmatrix},
$$


$$
[U,r]=
 \begin{pmatrix}(1+B+B^2)/D-B&B\\1+B-D&D\end{pmatrix}.
$$

Consequently the scheme of pairs of matrices with exactly these
shared entries, determinant one, and $D$ a unit is isomorphic
over $\Z$ to $B'_0\simeq\Gm\times\Ga$.
For a fixed chart point, all solutions of

$$
[\alpha,r]=[U,r],\qquad [\beta,s]=e[U,s],\quad e^2=1
$$

are uniquely

$$
\alpha=Ux^{-1},\qquad \beta=Uy^{-1},
 \quad x\in T_r,\quad y\in N'_s,\quad ys=e\,sy.
$$

This describes the stated chart and its fibres. It is not
an identification of this chart with the full group $M$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The two commutators are obtained by multiplying $U,r,s$
and their inverses. Conversely the common entries recover
$m=D^{-1}$ and $n=B/D$. The determinant equations recover
the upper-left entries, so no reducedness or field assumption
is used.
The first commutator equation is equivalent to
$\alpha^{-1}U$ commuting with $r$.
The second is equivalent to
$(\beta^{-1}U)s=e\,s(\beta^{-1}U)$.
Thus $x=\alpha^{-1}U$ and $y=\beta^{-1}U$ are the unique
parameters. All these identities are checked over the
universal Laurent polynomial ring by
\texttt{code/ab\_reconstructed.js}.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.7}{Proved}{Normalized modular triples over the requested bases}
Call a triple normalized if $\epsilon=-I$ and

$$
\det r_0=\det s_0=1,\quad
 \tr r_0=1,\quad\tr s_0=0,\quad
 \det(r_0+s_0)=0.
$$

Over $\Z$, over every field, and over $\Z/4,\Z/8,\Z/9$,
all such triples form one $\GL_2$-conjugacy orbit.
Their $\SL_2$-conjugacy classes are naturally indexed by
$R^\times/R^{\times2}$.
The relations $r_0^3=s_0^2=-I$ follow.
In characteristic two these are relations with $\epsilon=I$;
an additional requirement that $\epsilon$ have exact order two
would instead make that locus empty.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
A.3 applies with $u=1,v=-1,w=1$, giving the fixed matrices.
For the listed bases every rank-one projective module is free.
The matrix units in A.3 therefore arise from a basis of $R^2$,
so the algebraic pinning is induced by $\GL_2(R)$ conjugation.
The common centralizer is the scalar group because the two
matrices generate all of $M_2(R)$.
Hence the orbit is $\GL_2(R)/R^\times$ and its $\SL_2$-orbits
are the double quotient

$$
\SL_2(R)\backslash\GL_2(R)/R^\times
 \simeq R^\times/R^{\times2}.
$$

The determinant map is surjective and scalar determinants
are squares, proving the last isomorphism.
Cayley--Hamilton gives $r_0^2-r_0+I=0$ and $s_0^2+I=0$,
hence the asserted relations.
The square-class formulation also covers imperfect fields
of characteristic two; their units need not all be squares.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.8}{Proved}{The finite orbit census}
For the indicated rings, exhaustive enumeration gives

$$
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r}
R&\text{triples}&\SL_2\text{ orbits}&\text{size of each orbit}\\\hline
\F_2&6&1&6\\
\F_3&24&2&12\\
\F_5&120&2&60\\
\F_7&336&2&168\\
\F_{11}&1320&2&660\\
\F_{13}&2184&2&1092\\
\Z/4&48&2&24\\
\Z/8&384&4&96\\
\Z/9&648&2&324
\end{array}
$$

The signed chart of A.6 has respectively $12,576,1536$
distinct pairs over $\F_2,\F_3,\Z/4$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
A.7 gives the orbit count and orbit size
$|\SL_2(R)|/|\mu_2(R)|$. The replacement program independently
enumerates every determinant-one trace-one matrix and every
determinant-one trace-zero matrix, retaining precisely the
pairs with singular sum. It checks their total against
$|\SL_2(R)|$ and verifies the square-class partition.
For the signed chart it enumerates all $U,x,y$ and stores
the resulting ordered matrix pairs. Equality of the number
of distinct images with the number of parameters verifies
injectivity and the displayed cardinalities on those rings.
The universal inverse in A.6 supplies the all-ring proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.9}{Refuted}{Two invalid weakenings}
The algebraic pinning is not unique among all group-scheme
automorphisms of $\GL_2$. Also regularity of two unrelated
matrices and equality of their twisted commutators do not
suffice for the Borel parametrization of A.6.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The automorphism
$g\mapsto(\det g)^{-1}g$ is an involution of $\GL_2$ fixing
$\SL_2$ pointwise. It fixes $r,s$ but is not the identity,
for example on $\operatorname{diag}(2,1)$ over $\Q$.

For the second assertion work over $\F_5$, set $r'=s'=s$,
and take
$\alpha=\beta=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
The commutators are equal, and $r',s'$ are regular.
But $\alpha$ cannot belong to $B'_0T_s$: the lower row of
a matrix in that product is $(-d,c)$ with $c^2+d^2$ a unit,
whereas the lower row $(1,2)$ has norm $1+4=0$.
This obstruction persists after faithfully flat base change.
It refutes the weakened conditions just stated; it does not
remove the pinning and chart hypotheses in A.3 and A.6.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.10}{Unfinished}{The complete later symbol-by-symbol concordance}
The explicit core dictionary above and the formal constructions
in B.13--B.22 do not constitute a recovered, complete transcription
of every later symbol in §§48--49. The inaccessible original
working table included further entries and scan concordances.
Those entries cannot now be certified verbatim.
In particular, the source-specific restricted-normalizer
projections around C3-683--695 must not be declared surjective
merely from the formal fibre-product argument.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}[What is established and what is missing]
B.17 proves the exact reconstruction theorem with its explicit
equivariance and kernel hypotheses. B.18 gives a finite
counterexample to an unrestricted later normality assertion.
B.19--B.22 identify the exact image, cocycle, and diagonal-gluing
conditions required in the subsequent constructions.
The finite counterexample and the chart computations were
executed again in the surviving runtime.
What is missing from this fallback is the full recovered
source-specific list of restricted functors and a proof of
the required image equalities for its designated universal
profinite input. Accordingly the unrestricted dictionary and
all-formulas subrequests are not marked complete.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{A.11}{Proved}{A typed dictionary for the later formal constructions}
For group data $(C,L,B)$ with $B\subseteq\Aut(C)$ preserving $L$,
the construction of B.13 is a quotient group, or a profinite
quotient when all data are profinite and closed.
For equivariant data $(C,N,A,\phi)$ as in B.17 it is the
diagonal quotient $(C\rtimes N)/D_\phi$.
For maps to a common quotient $\Gamma$, the constructions in
B.19 and B.21 are fibre products and inverse-image subgroups.
For several marked extensions by $C$, the construction in B.22
is a diagonal subextension of their fibre product.
None of these statements alone asserts representability of an
arbitrary fppf sheaf quotient by an affine group scheme.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The exact hypotheses and proofs are given in the cited statements.
Quotients in groups, closed quotients in profinite groups, and
quotient fppf sheaves have different existence theorems.
The constructions provide the corresponding objects in the
categories explicitly named. Representability, where needed,
is proved separately for the finite-dimensional matrix schemes
of Part B and the affine inverse limits of Part E.
This distinction prevents a formal group calculation from being
used as an unproved representability or point-surjectivity claim.
\end{proof}

sections/B.tex
\section{The integral matrix scheme and the later formal constructions}

\begin{statement}{B.1}{Proved}{Equations and a scheme-theoretic factorization}
Let

$$
H=\Spec
 \frac{\Z[a,b,c,d,e,t,(c^2+d^2)^{-1}]}
 {(e^2-1,\ t^2-1,\ a^2+ab+b^2-et(c^2+d^2))}.
$$

Then $M$ is affine and

$$
M\simeq H\times\SL_2
$$

as schemes. The isomorphism sends $(h,g)$ to
$(x(h),y(h),g\operatorname{diag}(eq,1))$.
In particular the determinant relation in C3-399 is
$\det f=\det y=t\det x$, not equality of three unrelated
norm-one conditions.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The equations defining $H$ are exactly the first two matrix
coordinates and their determinant compatibility. Since
$e,t,q$ are units, so is $n=etq$.
A matrix $f$ of determinant $eq$ factors uniquely as
$g\operatorname{diag}(eq,1)$ with $g\in\SL_2$.
The factorization and its inverse are regular over the displayed
coordinate ring. This proves the scheme assertion over $\Z$,
including all nonreduced test rings.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.2}{Proved}{Integral flatness and dimension}
The group scheme $M$ is flat and finitely presented over $\Z$,
of relative dimension six. The three defining polynomials of
$H$, before localization, form a monic Groebner basis with
leading monomials $e^2,t^2,a^2$ for a suitable lexicographic
order. This is an integral certificate, not a computation
over $\Q$ followed by clearing denominators.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Take the variable order $a>e>t>b>c>d$.
The three leading coefficients are one and the leading
monomials are pairwise relatively prime. The product criterion,
or direct division of their three S-polynomials, gives zero
remainders over $\Z$. The residue monomials have exponents
at most one in $a,e,t$, with arbitrary exponents in $b,c,d$.
They form a free $\Z$-basis. Localizing at $q=c^2+d^2$
preserves $\Z$-flatness.
Every fibre has dimension three: the two sign coordinates
are finite, and the monic equation in $a$ leaves a finite
algebra over the three polynomial coordinates $b,c,d$.
The nonempty unit locus still has dimension three.
Multiplication by the smooth three-dimensional scheme $\SL_2$
in B.1 proves the assertion for $M$.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.3}{Proved}{The smoothness locus and the connected kernel}
The scheme $M$ is smooth exactly over $\Spec\Z[1/2]$.
The subgroup $M^{00}=\ker(e,t)$ is smooth over all of $\Z$,
with geometrically connected fibres. It is therefore a
normal integral scheme. The map

$$
M\longrightarrow V=\mu_2^2,\qquad m\longmapsto(e,t)
$$

is an fppf surjection with kernel $M^{00}$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Away from two the signs are etale. In the remaining equation,
the derivatives in $c,d$ are $-2etc,-2etd$.
They cannot vanish simultaneously on $q$ invertible.
The standard smoothness criterion therefore applies
\cite[Lemma10.137.6]{AB_Stacks}.

For $M^{00}$ the equation is $n=q$.
In characteristic two, its derivatives in $a,b$ are $b,a$,
and these cannot both vanish because $n=q$ is a unit.
In characteristic three, simultaneous vanishing of
$2a+b,a+2b$ forces $a=b$, hence $n=0$, again impossible.
In every other characteristic, the same conclusion follows
from invertibility of three, or from the derivatives in $c,d$.
Thus $M^{00}$ is smooth over $\Z$.

Over an algebraically closed field of characteristic different
from two and three, its scalar factor is
$x_1x_2=y_1y_2$ with all four variables units, hence a
three-dimensional torus. In characteristic three it is
$\Ga\times\Gm^2$: the Eisenstein norm is the square of a
linear coordinate, while the Gaussian algebra is split.
In characteristic two it is also $\Ga\times\Gm^2$ after
splitting the etale Eisenstein algebra.
Each description is connected, as is $\SL_2$.
Smoothness over the regular base and the integral generic
fibre imply normality and integrality of the total scheme.

For any prescribed signs, fppf locally choose square roots
of their required unit norms; scalar matrices then supply
$x,y$, and choose a diagonal $f$ of the resulting determinant.
This proves surjectivity onto $V$. Each fibre is a torsor
under the smooth kernel, so the quotient map is smooth
and fppf. B.4 shows that $M$ itself is not smooth at two.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.4}{Proved}{The characteristic-two fibre}
The geometric characteristic-two fibre of $M$ has one
irreducible component, dimension six, multiplicity four,
and tangent dimension eight at every geometric point.
Its reduced group is

$$
\SL_2\times ST_r\times\Gm\times\Ga
$$

over $\F_2$. The whole characteristic-two fibre is singular
as a six-dimensional fibre scheme.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
In characteristic two,
$e^2-1=(e-1)^2$ and $t^2-1=(t-1)^2$.
Their differentials vanish. The norm equation has nonzero
differential in the $a,b$ directions on the unit locus.
Hence the scalar tangent dimension is five, rather than
three, and the $\SL_2$ factor adds three.
The sign base $\mu_2^2$ has local length four.
The smooth quotient map of B.3 shows that the fibre is a
smooth deformation over this length-four local base of its
reduction; equivalently its completed local rings have two
independent square-zero sign parameters and six smooth
parameters. This proves the multiplicity.

On the reduction $e=t=1$. Put $z=c+d$, a unit, and write
$x=z x_0$. Then $N_r(x_0)=1$, while $d/z$ is an arbitrary
additive coordinate. These substitutions and their inverses
give the displayed reduced group scheme.
The Eisenstein norm-one group is a connected torus at two,
so the reduced fibre is geometrically irreducible.
The tangent computation proves singularity at every point.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.5}{Proved}{The characteristic-three fibre}
The geometric characteristic-three fibre of $M$ has four
smooth irreducible components. Each is isomorphic as a
scheme to

$$
\SL_2\times\Ga\times\Gm^2.
$$

Thus three is not a bad smoothness prime for this particular
six-dimensional scheme.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The two signs are etale and give four components.
In characteristic three, $n=(a-b)^2$.
After extending the field to split the Gaussian algebra,
write $q=uv$, with $u,v$ units.
For fixed signs the equation is $z^2=etuv$, where
$z=a-b$ is a unit. It eliminates $v$ as $et z^2/u$;
$b$ is free. This gives $\Ga\times\Gm^2$.
The factorization in B.1 completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.6}{Proved}{The norm-one groups have different bad fibres}
The group $ST_r$ is smooth over all of $\Z$.
Its geometric fibre at three is the disjoint union of two
copies of $\Ga$. The group $ST_s$ is nonreduced at two:
there its equation is $(c+d-1)^2=0$.
Neither fact contradicts smoothness of $M^{00}$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
For $ST_r$, the equation is $a^2+ab+b^2=1$.
The gradient analysis in B.3 proves smoothness at every prime.
At three it becomes $(a-b)^2=1$, giving the two affine lines.
For $ST_s$ the equation is $c^2+d^2=1$, which at two has
the displayed square form. Away from two its gradient is
nonzero. In $M^{00}$, the Gaussian norm is equated to an
independent Eisenstein norm rather than to a fixed constant;
the latter provides the missing derivative at two.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.7}{Proved}{The main quotient and its exact kernel}
The homomorphism

$$
\Psi:M\longrightarrow\GL_2\times\mu_2^2,\qquad
 (x,y,f)\longmapsto(f,e,t)
$$

is fppf surjective, with kernel
$K=ST_r\times ST_s$.
This kernel is central in $M^{00}$; it is not central in
the full group $M$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
For target data $(f,e,t)$, put $\delta=\det f$.
One needs $N_r(x)=t\delta$ and $N_s(c-di)=e\delta$.
Adjoining square roots of these two units gives finite
locally free covers on which scalar solutions exist.
This proves fppf surjectivity. Setting $f=I,e=t=1$
gives exactly the two norm-one factors.
Inside $M^{00}$ both centralizer factors are commutative.
The $e=-1$ component conjugates the Gaussian factor by its
nontrivial algebra involution, which is inversion on $ST_s$.
That action is nontrivial, so centrality fails in $M$.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.8}{Proved}{The projective kernel and central-product presentation}
Put

$$
H'=\{(x_0,y_0):x_0\in T_r,\ N_r(x_0)^2=1,\
                 y_0\in N'_s,\ \det y_0=1\}.
$$

Then

$$
R_M=\ker(M\to\PGL_2)\simeq\Gm\times H'.
$$

There is an fppf exact central-product sequence

$$
1\longrightarrow\mu_2\longrightarrow R_M\times\SL_2
 \longrightarrow M\longrightarrow1,
$$

where the kernel is the antidiagonal scalar copy of $\mu_2$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
In the projective kernel, $f=\lambda I$ uniquely.
Write $x=\lambda x_0$, $y=\lambda y_0$.
The determinant conditions become
$N_r(x_0)=t\in\mu_2$ and $\det y_0=1$.
This gives the asserted product, with its explicit inverse.
The embedded $\SL_2$ occupies only the third matrix factor
and commutes with $R_M$.
Given $m=(x,y,f)$, fppf locally choose $\lambda^2=\det f$.
Then $(x,y,\lambda I)\in R_M$ and
$\lambda^{-1}f\in\SL_2$, proving surjectivity.
The product is the identity exactly when $x=y=I$ and the
two third factors are inverse scalar matrices with square one.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.9}{Proved}{The two centres}
The centres as group schemes are

$$
Z(M^{00})=\Gm\times ST_r\times ST_s,
 \qquad
 Z(M)=\Gm\times S'T_r\times\mu_2.
$$

In particular $Z(M^{00})$ is not the scalar-coordinate group
$H^{00}$ from B.1. Over $\Z$ their point counts are
$48$ and $24$, respectively.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Commutation with the embedded $\SL_2$ forces the third
matrix to be scalar, say $\lambda I$.
In $M^{00}$ both other factors commute, and the norm equations
give $x=\lambda x_0$, $y=\lambda y_0$ with both $x_0,y_0$
of norm one. This gives the first formula.

For the full group, a central $y$ must be scalar.
Indeed commutation with the universal Gaussian unit forces
its sign to be one, and commutation with the universal sign
over $R[e]/(e^2-1)$ forces its off-diagonal coefficient to
vanish. This last universal test is necessary: checking only
the two ordinary signs would lose information at two.
Write $y=\lambda\kappa I$. Then $\kappa^2=1$ and
$N_r(x/\lambda)\in\mu_2$, giving the second formula.
Conversely these elements commute with every factor.

The integral norm-one units have orders six and four.
Thus the first centre has $2\cdot6\cdot4=48$ integral points.
In $H^{00}(\Z)$ positivity forces both norms to one,
giving only $6\cdot4=24$ points.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.10}{Proved}{Graph subgroups and the true line stabilizer}
Let $\chi=\det f=eq$ on $H$.
The graph $f=\operatorname{diag}(\chi,1)$ defines a subgroup
$M(0)\simeq H$. The inverse image $M[0]$ of $B'_0$ has
dimension four. The graph $f=y$ defines $M(s)\simeq H$.
The graph $f=x$ is available only on the locus $t=1$.
The full stabilizer of the first coordinate line under
$M\to\GL_2$ is the inverse image of $B_0$, of dimension five;
it is strictly larger than $M[0]$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Each displayed graph is a graph of a group homomorphism.
For $f=x$, equality $\det f=t\det x$ forces $t=1$,
because $\det x$ is a unit.
In $B'_0$, the determinant specifies the upper-left entry,
leaving one free upper-right entry over $H$.
The full upper triangular group also has an arbitrary
bottom-right unit, leaving two parameters over $H$.
For example $\operatorname{diag}(2,1/2)$ over $\Q$ fixes
the first coordinate line but does not belong to $B'_0$.
Thus a line-normalizer statement cannot substitute $M[0]$
for the full inverse image of $B_0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.11}{Proved}{The seven-dimensional auxiliary scheme}
The fibre product

$$
SM=M\times_{\chi,\Gm,\det}B'_0
$$

is affine, flat over $\Z$, and has relative dimension seven.
Its projection to $M$ is a split smooth morphism with kernel
$\Ga$. It is not $M^+=\ker\chi$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The determinant map $B'_0\to\Gm$ is the projection
$(m,n)\mapsto m$ and has the section $m\mapsto(m,0)$.
Its base change has the stated properties and is isomorphic
as a scheme to $M\times\A^1$.
It adds a Borel parameter instead of imposing determinant one.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.12}{Proved}{Exact finite counts}
The scalar scheme and matrix group have the following point counts:

$$
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r|r}
R&|H(R)|&|\SL_2(R)|&|M(R)|&|M^{00}(R)|\\\hline
\F_2&6&6&36&36\\
\F_3&96&24&2304&576\\
\F_5&384&120&46080&11520\\
\F_7&1152&336&387072&96768\\
\F_{11}&5760&1320&7603200&1900800\\
\F_{13}&6912&2184&15095808&3773952\\
\Z/4&192&48&9216&2304\\
\Z/8&6144&384&2359296&147456\\
\Z/9&2592&648&1679616&419904
\end{array}
$$

These are counts of ring-valued points, not fibre multiplicities.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
For each unit $q$, enumerate the Gaussian norm fibre and
the Eisenstein norm fibre at $etq$, and sum over all actual
solutions of $e^2=t^2=1$ in the ring.
For $H^{00}$ use $e=t=1$.
Multiply by $|\SL_2(R)|$ using B.1.
The replacement program enumerates these finite sets exactly.
Its simultaneous triple count from A.8 independently agrees
with the special-linear factor in every row.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.13}{Proved}{The cusp quotient and its central ambiguity}
Let $C$ be a group, let $L\subseteq C$ be abelian, and let
$B\subseteq\Aut(C)$ preserve $L$.
In $C\rtimes B$ the subgroup

$$
D_L=\{(\ell^{-1},\Int\ell):\ell\in L\}
$$

is normal and centralizes the copy of $C$.
The quotient $G=(C\rtimes B)/D_L$ contains $B$ injectively;
the kernel of $C\to G$ is exactly $L\cap Z(C)$.
Its intersection between the images of $B$ and $C$ is
the image of $L$.

For profinite data with $L,B$ closed and all maps continuous,
these are profinite quotients. If an automorphism of a
profinite group satisfies $u(L)\subseteq L$ for procyclic
$L$, then in fact $u(L)=L$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Multiplication and conjugation show that the displayed graph
is a subgroup, that it centralizes $C$, and that $B$ conjugates
it into itself. Its intersection with $C$ consists of precisely
the central elements of $L$, whereas its intersection with
$B$ is trivial. The equation
$(\ell,1)=(1,\Int\ell)$ in the quotient proves the intersection
assertion.
For profinite groups the graph is compact and closed.

For the final assertion write $L=\overline{\langle\lambda\rangle}$
and $u(\lambda)=\lambda^a$ for a profinite exponent $a$.
If multiplication by $a$ were not onto $L$, some prime $p$
would divide $a$ on a nontrivial $p$-primary part of $L$.
But
$\lambda=(u^{-n}\lambda)^{a^n}$
for every $n$. In every finite quotient, sufficiently large
$n$ kills the $p$-part of this power. Hence the image of
$\lambda$ has trivial $p$-part in every finite quotient,
a contradiction. Thus multiplication by $a$ is onto $L$.
For finitely generated profinite $C$, characteristic finite
quotients give the usual compact topology on $\Aut(C)$;
the cusp stabilizer is closed. Continuous bijections between
the resulting compact quotients and Hausdorff target groups
are homeomorphisms.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.14}{Proved}{Point images are norm conditions}
A triple $(f,e,t)$ belongs to $\Psi(M(R))$ if and only if

$$
t\det f\in N_r(T_r(R)),\qquad
 e\det f\in N_s(T_s(R)).
$$

Thus fppf surjectivity in B.7 does not imply pointwise
surjectivity. For the requested small rings the conditions are:

$$
\begin{array}{c|l}
R&\text{condition}\\\hline
\Z&e=t=\det f\in\{1,-1\}\\
\F_2&\text{none}\\
\F_3&t=\det f,\quad e\text{ arbitrary}\\
\Z/4&e=\det f,\quad t\text{ arbitrary}.
\end{array}
$$

\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Necessity is the defining determinant equation, and independent
solutions of the two norm equations give sufficiency.
Over $\Z$ both unit norms are one by positivity.
Over $\F_3$ the Eisenstein unit norm is one and the Gaussian
norm is onto. Over $\Z/4$ the Gaussian unit norm is one and
the Eisenstein norm is onto the two unit classes.
The finite enumerations in B.12 verify all these norm images.
D.2 gives the complete local and adelic versions.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.15}{Proved}{The pinning family with a unit parameter}
For a unit $v$, replace the fixed pair by

$$
r_v=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\v&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 s_v=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\-v&0\end{pmatrix}.
$$

The two norm polynomials become

$$
n_v=a^2+ab-vb^2,\qquad q_v=c^2-vd^2.
$$

The corresponding matrix group is flat of relative dimension
six, smooth away from two; its kernel with both signs one
is smooth over the entire unit-parameter base.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Direct multiplication gives
$r_v^2=r_v+vI$, $s_v^2=vI$, and $r_v+s_v=E_{22}$.
The centralizer and signed-normalizer matrices have determinants
$n_v$ and $e q_v$. The monic basis argument of B.2 is unchanged.
Away from two the derivatives in $c,d$ cannot vanish on
$q_v$ invertible because $v$ is a unit.
At two the derivatives in $a,b$ are $b,a$ and the unit norm
excludes their simultaneous vanishing.
These identities are included in the universal polynomial
verification program.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.16}{Proved}{The two adelic projective quotients differ}
The natural map

$$
\GL_2(\widehat\Z)/\{\pm I\}_{\rm diagonal}
 \longrightarrow(\GL_2/\mu_2)(\widehat\Z)
$$

is neither injective nor surjective. Its kernel is

$$
\frac{\prod_p\{\pm1\}}{\{\pm1\}_{\rm diagonal}},
$$

and its cokernel is
$\widehat\Z^\times/\widehat\Z^{\times2}$.
Consequently a requested passage between later profinite groups
cannot treat these two quotients as identical.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Every finite-rank constant-rank projective module over
$\widehat\Z=\prod_p\Z_p$ is free: represent it by an idempotent
matrix, choose a basis and inverse basis on each component,
and assemble those matrices in the product ring.
In particular $\operatorname{Pic}(\widehat\Z)=0$ and all
rank-two vector bundles are trivial.
The Kummer exact sequence and the central quotient sequence give

$$
\mu_2(\widehat\Z)\to\GL_2(\widehat\Z)
 \to(\GL_2/\mu_2)(\widehat\Z)
 \to H^1(\widehat\Z,\mu_2)\to H^1(\widehat\Z,\GL_2).
$$

The last set is trivial and the preceding group is
$\widehat\Z^\times/\widehat\Z^{\times2}$
\cite[Lemma59.28.3]{AB_Stacks}.
Moreover $\mu_2(\widehat\Z)=\prod_p\{\pm1\}$.
Dividing only by the two diagonal signs gives the kernel stated.
Both that kernel and the square-class quotient are nontrivial.
This calculation supplies an obstruction, not a construction
of every marked map between the later tower groups.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.17}{Proved}{The general reconstruction theorem}
Let $\theta:N\to\Aut(C)$, let $A\triangleleft N$, and let
$\phi:A\to C$ be a homomorphism satisfying

$$
\theta_a=\Int(\phi(a)),\qquad
 \phi(nan^{-1})=\theta_n(\phi(a)).
$$

Then

$$
D_\phi=\{(\phi(a)^{-1},a):a\in A\}
 \triangleleft C\rtimes_\theta N
$$

and $G=(C\rtimes N)/D_\phi$ fits into

$$
1\to C\to G\to N/A\to1.
$$

The map $N\to G$ has kernel $\ker\phi$.
The same assertion holds for profinite groups with closed
continuous data, and for fppf group sheaves as a sheaf quotient.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The inner-action identity shows that the graph is a subgroup
and centralizes $C$. The equivariance identity gives its
normality under $N$. Its intersection with $C$ is trivial,
and its intersection with $N$ is $\ker\phi$.
Every element with second coordinate in $A$ becomes an
element of $C$, proving the exact sequence.
All operations are continuous in the profinite case, and
the graph is closed. The same elementwise identities are
identities of sheaves. They do not on their own prove
representability of a general sheaf quotient.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.18}{Refuted}{The unrestricted normality assertion}
In a split extension $C\rtimes\Gamma$, the complementary
copy of $\Gamma$ need not be normal.
A counterexample compatible with the modular finite quotient
is

$$
C=\SL_2(\F_3),\quad
 \Gamma=\widehat\Z^\times,\quad
 \theta_\gamma=\Int(\operatorname{diag}(-1,1))
       \text{ when }\gamma\bmod3=-1.
$$

The finite quotient is $\GL_2(\F_3)$ of order48,
and the complementary order-two subgroup is not normal.
A separate central-intersection issue already occurs for
a cyclic order-four cusp in $\operatorname{Dic}_3$.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Let $J=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1)$ and
$c=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$
over $\F_3$. Then $JcJ^{-1}=c^{-1}\ne c$.
In $C\rtimes C_2$, conjugating the complement generator by
$c$ gives a nontrivial $C$-coordinate, so leaves the complement.
The determinant sequence identifies this semidirect product
with $\GL_2(\F_3)$.
The modular subgroup generated by the two squared cusp
generators has full image $\SL_2(\F_3)$: their reductions
are upper and lower elementary matrices with nonzero
parameters. Thus triviality of the induced action is a real
condition, not a consequence of the modular relations.

More generally, when $C_0\triangleleft C$ is stable and the
relevant cusp is already contained in $C_0$, the subgroup
$C_0\rtimes\Gamma$ is normal exactly when $\Gamma$ acts
trivially on $C/C_0$. This follows by computing
$(c,1)(1,\gamma)(c^{-1},1)$.
If the cusp is not contained in $C_0$, its actual intersection
must instead be retained in the diagonal quotient.
For $\operatorname{Dic}_3=C_3\rtimes C_4$, an order-four
cusp has central square. Its inner image has order two,
while its intersection with the trivial subgroup is trivial.
Replacing one of those kernels by the other therefore
changes the reconstruction.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.19}{Proved}{The master fibre-product exactness calculation}
Let $A\to\Gamma$, $B\to\Gamma$, and $G\to\Gamma$ be
homomorphisms. Their iterated fibre product is

$$
P=\{(a,b,g):\alpha(a)=\beta(b)=\gamma(g)\}.
$$

If $A\to\Gamma$ and $B\to\Gamma$ are epimorphisms in
the category in use, then $P\to G$ is an epimorphism with
kernel $\ker\alpha\times\ker\beta$.
Without those hypotheses its image is exactly

$$
\gamma^{-1}(\im\alpha\cap\im\beta).
$$

\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The kernel formula follows by setting $g=1$.
A point has a lift precisely when its common quotient value
lifts to both $A$ and $B$. For sheaves the lifts may be
chosen on a common refinement of the two covers, giving
the same sheaf-image formula.
This proves the formal exact sequences used in the
fibre-product constructions around C3-559--562 and
C3-673--681 whenever the stated epimorphism hypotheses hold.
It also identifies exactly what must be checked before
asserting their surjectivity for restricted subgroups.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.20}{Proved}{The affine-cocycle obstruction to a diagonal section}
Let $N$ map to an affine group $A\rtimes\Gamma$, with
translation coordinate $\ell$ and a normal subgroup $L$
mapping to translations $jL$. The induced translation
class on the quotient takes values in $A/jL$.
The image of the diagonal subgroup is exactly the zero
locus of this class. A prescribed diagonal section exists
only if this class is zero.
Even with trivial linear action, the extension can fail
to have any section.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
An affine element can be made diagonal by multiplication
with an allowed translation precisely when its translation
coordinate belongs to $jL$. This is the stated condition
in the quotient module.
For an explicit nonsplit example use

$$
0\longrightarrow2\widehat\Z\longrightarrow\widehat\Z
 \longrightarrow C_2\longrightarrow0,
$$

represented by translations of the affine line.
The nontrivial quotient class has odd translation coordinate.
A section would require an odd element of order two in
the torsion-free group $\widehat\Z$, which is impossible.
The same obstruction is already visible in
$0\to2(\Z/4)\to\Z/4\to C_2\to0$.
Thus a Borel realization does not automatically supply the
particular diagonal splitting requested by a later diagram.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.21}{Proved}{Restricted functors and their exact images}
An automorphism of $C$ descends to $C/K$ exactly when it
preserves $K$ as a subgroup, not merely as an unnamed
isomorphism type. If $A'\subseteq A$ and $B'\subseteq B$
are restrictions in B.19, their fibre-product projection
has image

$$
\gamma^{-1}(\im A'\cap\im B')
$$

and kernel $\ker(A'\to\Gamma)\times\ker(B'\to\Gamma)$.
These formulas do not establish that the source-specific
restricted projections on C3-690 are epimorphisms.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
A quotient map factors through $C/K$ exactly when it kills
$K$. Applying the same assertion to the inverse automorphism
gives equality of the preserved subgroup.
The fibre-product image and kernel follow from B.19 with
the restricted domains. In the profinite case one retains
closed subgroups and continuous maps; in the sheaf case one
uses sheaf images. The missing source-specific assertion
is precisely equality of these images with the required
quotient image. No such equality is inferred from a notation
for the fibre product.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.22}{Proved}{Diagonal gluing of marked extensions}
Let $G_i\to\Gamma$ be extensions with the same marked kernel
$C$, and let $P=\prod_\Gamma G_i$.
A subgroup $H\subseteq P$ gives simultaneous compatible
identifications of the extensions exactly when

$$
H\to\Gamma\text{ is an epimorphism},\qquad
 H\cap C^I=\Delta C.
$$

Then every projection $H\to G_i$ is an isomorphism.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The kernel of a projection lies in $\Delta C$ and has
identity $i$th coordinate, so is trivial.
Given $g_i$, lift its quotient value to an element of $H$.
Their difference in the $i$th coordinate belongs to $C$;
multiplication by that diagonal element corrects the
coordinate. This proves surjectivity, locally in the
sheaf setting. Conversely compatible identifications have
a graph with precisely these properties.
The transition identifications obey the cocycle relation
because they arise from projections of one common group.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{B.23}{Proved}{The finer parameter scheme on C4-41}
Use the earlier visible convention $\nu=2h+i$ from C4-38,
with constant bits $i,j\in\{0,1\}$.
Put

$$
R=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 S=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
 \alpha=(a,b),\quad\beta=(c,d).
$$

The equations on C4-39--41 become

$$
a+b-c+d=0,\qquad 3b-2h=i+j,\qquad
 4h+2i+1\text{ invertible}.
$$

Each bit chart is

$$
\Spec\Z[c,d,w,(1-12w+6i+4j)^{-1}],
 \qquad w=b-h.
$$

The complete scheme is therefore smooth and flat of relative
dimension three over all of $\Z$, including two and three.
Its fibres at those two primes are four affine three-spaces.
The bits here are constant components, not $\mu_2$ parameters.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Put $k=i+j$. The integral change of variables is

$$
b=k-2w,\qquad h=k-3w,\qquad
 a=c-d-k+2w.
$$

It is inverse to $w=b-h$ on the defining equations.
The invertible multiplier becomes
$1-12w+6i+4j$.
This is an integral unimodular calculation; a saturated
Groebner basis over $\Q$ would not suffice.
At two the multiplier is one, and at three it is $1+j$,
always a unit. Thus all four bad-prime charts are affine
three-spaces. For primes at least five, one value of $w$
is excluded in each chart, giving $4p^2(p-1)$ points.

The point counts, independently enumerated, are
$32,108,400,1176,4840,8112$ at
$p=2,3,5,7,11,13$, and $256,2048,2916$ over
$\Z/4,\Z/8,\Z/9$.
Over $\Z$, the only possible bit and $w$ values are
$(i,j,w)=(0,0,0)$ and $(1,1,1)$, with $c,d$ arbitrary,
because an integral unit multiplier is $1$ or $-1$.

For completeness replace the right side by
$2h+s_2i+s_3j$, $s_2,s_3\in\{\pm1\}$.
The same calculation gives multiplier
$1-12w+6s_2i+4s_3j$.
All charts remain smooth. If $s_3=-1,j=1$, their fibre
at three is empty; this is a substantive change.
Changing the convention for the $i$-sign is instead
absorbed by $h_-=h_++i$, $w_-=w_+-i$.
The unaugmented linear map in $(a,b,c,d)$ has Smith
invariants $1,3$; the coefficient two of $h$ removes that
cokernel. This explains why failure of that smaller map
to be onto at three does not make the finer scheme singular.
\end{proof}

sections/F.tex
\section{F. Low types, orientation, and a non-orientable test}

\begin{statement}{F.1}{Proved}{Low orientable types, peripheral structures, and centres}
For an orientable surface of genus $g$ with $n$ punctures and
$2g-2+n\leq0$, the possible types and discrete fundamental groups are

$$
(0,0):1,\qquad (0,1):1,\qquad (0,2):\mathbb Z,\qquad
 (1,0):\mathbb Z^2.
$$

Each group equals its centre. For $r=0,1,2$,

$$
\operatorname{Env}(\mathbb Z^r)_{\mathbb Q}
 =\mathbb G_a^r\times\widehat{\mathbb Z}^{\,r}_{\mathbb Q},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Env}(\mathbb Z^r)_{\mathbb F_p}
 =\widehat{\mathbb Z}^{\,r}_{\mathbb F_p}.
$$

The finite lattice stage for $\mathbb Z^r$ is $\mathbb G_a^r$.
For a finite group $F$, $\operatorname{Env}(F)=\underline F$.
In particular the projective-plane group gives
$\operatorname{Env}(C_2)=\underline{C_2}$, not $\mu_2$.

The types $(0,3)$ and $(1,1)$ both have discrete group $F_2$ and
trivial centre, but have different peripheral markings. The first
has three peripheral conjugacy classes represented by
$x,y,(xy)^{-1}$; the second has one represented by $[x,y]$,
up to the orientation convention. A comparison which forgets
these markings does not distinguish the two types.
These statements qualify the low types; they do not establish a
general anabelian reconstruction theorem.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
The inequality gives exactly the four listed pairs. A punctured
sphere retracts to a bouquet of $n-1$ circles when $n>0$.
The sphere is simply connected, and the torus cell decomposition
gives $\langle a,b:[a,b]=1\rangle=\mathbb Z^2$.
The first centre assertions follow immediately.

For a lattice, apply E.12 to $n\mathbb Z^r$. Its components are
indexed by $(\mathbb Z/n)^r and have coordinates representing
$a+nT$. Over $\mathbb Q$, $X=a+nT$ gives compatible isomorphisms
with $\mathbb G_a^r\times(\mathbb Z/n)^r$; taking the inverse
limit proves the generic-fibre formula. In characteristic $p$,
refinement from $n$ to $pn$ makes each polynomial coordinate
constant on the finer components, giving the profinite constant
fibre as in E.14. For finite $F$, the zero subgroup is its only
torsion-free abelian subgroup, so the lattice refinement system
has that single object. The standard cell presentation of the
projective plane has relation $a^2=1$. Constant $C_2$ is etale
at two, whereas $\mu_2$ is not.

The thrice-punctured sphere has the presentation
$\langle x,y,z:xyz=1\rangle$ and hence free group on $x,y$.
Removing a disc from a torus removes its commutator relation
and leaves a boundary curve represented by $[x,y]$.
The centralizer of a free generator $x$ in $F(x,y)$ is
$\langle x\rangle$: a reduced word containing a $y$-syllable
cannot commute with $x$, by comparing reduced normal forms.
The analogous assertion for $y$ and
$\langle x\rangle\cap\langle y\rangle=1$ prove that the centre
is trivial. The peripheral markings follow from the respective
cell decompositions. Quotienting any of the abelian low-type
groups by its centre would instead discard the whole group.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{F.2}{Proved}{Orientation reversal and the full manuscript group}
Let $\Pi_0,S$ be as in E.18 and put $J=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1)$.
Then

$$
S^\pm:=\operatorname{GL}_2(\mathbb Z)/[\Pi_0,\Pi_0]
       =S\rtimes C_2
$$

as a discrete group, with involution induced by conjugation by $J$.
The full manuscript finite quotient has order $24$, whereas that
for $S$ has order $12$. For the induced action on envelopes,

$$
\operatorname{Env}(S^\pm)
   =\operatorname{Env}(S)\rtimes\underline{C_2}.
$$

The same assertion holds for the finite lattice stage.
This does not assert an integral splitting of the lattice extension
by its order-$24$ quotient.

On real matrix points orientation is controlled by
$\operatorname{sgn}(\det)$; positive determinant is different from
determinant one. The real sign map is not asserted to be a
morphism of group schemes.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Direct multiplication gives $J\lambda_iJ^{-1}=\lambda_i^{-1}$
for $i=0,1$. Thus $[\Pi_0,\Pi_0]$ is preserved by $J$ and by
$\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ and is normal in
$\operatorname{GL}_2(\mathbb Z)$. The determinant map on the
latter has image $\{\pm1\}$ and section $-1\mapsto J$.
Taking the quotient gives the split orientation extension.
Its index-two subgroup has finite lattice quotient of order
$12$, so the full quotient has order $24$.

The sublattices $n\mathbb Z^2$ are stable under the involution.
Apply E.12 at each such stage and take the inverse limit.
The constant $C_2$ factor and its section persist.
The orientation section need not preserve the chosen finite
subgroup used to split E.18, so this argument does not supply
a section of the order-$24$ lattice quotient.

For the standard alternating form $\omega$ on a real plane,
$\omega(Av,Aw)=\det(A)\omega(v,w)$ by direct expansion.
An antiholomorphic involution of a complex torus reverses
orientation, and therefore has determinant $-1$ on integral
first homology, by the intersection pairing. This explains
the need for an orientation-reversing operator in a
general-linear comparison; it does not identify a real
arithmetic fundamental group with this split modular quotient.
The orientation group is constant $C_2$ throughout.
Replacing it by $\mu_2$ at two would change the group scheme.
\end{proof}

\begin{statement}{F.3}{Proved}{A nonsplit integral model for the Klein bottle}
Let

$$
K=\langle a,b:aba^{-1}=b^{-1}\rangle,\qquad
 L=\langle a^2,b\rangle\simeq\mathbb Z^2.
$$

Then $K/L=C_2$, the centre is $\langle a^2\rangle$, and the
finite lattice model of E.12 is the disjoint union of two
affine planes with multiplication

$$
(x,y,\epsilon)(x',y',\delta)=
 (x+x'+\epsilon\delta,\ y+(-1)^\epsilon y',\
       \epsilon+\delta\pmod2),
 \qquad \epsilon,\delta\in\{0,1\}.
$$

This extension by $\mathbb G_a^2$ has no section of its constant
$C_2$ quotient over $\mathbb Z$ or $\mathbb F_2$.
It splits over $\mathbb Z[1/2]$.
Thus even this non-orientable Euler-characteristic-zero case
requires the cocycle construction rather than an assumption
of an integral splitting.
\end{statement}
\begin{proof}
Normal forms $a^m b^n$ follow by moving each $a$ past each $b$.
They are distinct in the plane action

$$
a(x,y)=(x+1/2,-y),\qquad b(x,y)=(x,y+1).
$$

Thus $L$ is free abelian of rank two and has index two.
Conjugation by $a$ fixes $a^2$ and inverts $b$.
Choosing $a$ as representative of the nontrivial coset gives
$c(1,1)=(1,0)$, and E.12 gives the displayed group law.
To commute with $b$, a normal form must have even $a$ exponent;
to commute also with $a$, its $b$ exponent must be zero.
This proves the centre assertion.

Every point in the nonidentity component has square
$(2x+1,0,0)$. Over $\mathbb Z$ this is never the identity;
over $\mathbb F_2$ it is always $(1,0,0)$.
A section would require an element of order two in that
component. Over $\mathbb Z[1/2]$, $(-1/2,0,1)$ supplies one.

On $\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+i\mathbb Z)$ the involution
$z\mapsto\overline z+1/2$ is antiholomorphic and fixed-point-free:
its real displacement cannot be an integer. Its square is
translation by one, hence the identity on the torus.
The quotient is the Klein bottle, with the displayed plane
transformations as deck generators. This supplies a concrete
orientation-reversing example and its finite lattice model.
It does not establish comparisons for arbitrary real
arithmetic actions or all non-orientable hyperbolic surfaces.
\end{proof}

code/ab_reconstructed.js
/* Reconstructed A/B verification; no external packages.
   Actually executed in the V8 tool runtime on 2026-11-11.
   Node: node ab_reconstructed.js */
const output=[];
function log(s){output.push(String(s));}
function assert(b,s){if(!b)throw new Error(s);}
const names=["a","b","c","d","e","t","m","n","v"],nv=names.length;
function mon(c,ex=[]){const a=Array(nv).fill(0);ex.forEach((z,i)=>a[i]=z);return c===0n?new Map():new Map([[a.join(","),BigInt(c)]]);}
const zero=()=>new Map(),one=()=>mon(1n);
function variable(i,k=1){const a=Array(nv).fill(0);a[i]=k;return mon(1n,a);}
function add(...ps){const r=new Map();for(const p of ps)for(const[k,c]of p){const v=(r.get(k)||0n)+c;if(v)r.set(k,v);else r.delete(k);}return r;}
function neg(p){return new Map([...p].map(([k,c])=>[k,-c]));}
function sub(a,b){return add(a,neg(b));}
function mul(...ps){let r=one();for(const p of ps){const s=new Map();for(const[ka,ca]of r)for(const[kb,cb]of p){const aa=ka.split(",").map(Number),bb=kb.split(",").map(Number),cc=aa.map((x,i)=>x+bb[i]);cc[4]=((cc[4]%2)+2)%2;cc[5]=((cc[5]%2)+2)%2;const k=cc.join(","),z=(s.get(k)||0n)+ca*cb;if(z)s.set(k,z);else s.delete(k);}r=s;}return r;}
function eq(a,b){return sub(a,b).size===0;}
function matrix(a,b,c,d){return[a,b,c,d];}
function mm(A,B){return[add(mul(A[0],B[0]),mul(A[1],B[2])),add(mul(A[0],B[1]),mul(A[1],B[3])),add(mul(A[2],B[0]),mul(A[3],B[2])),add(mul(A[2],B[1]),mul(A[3],B[3]))];}
function ma(A,B){return A.map((v,i)=>add(v,B[i]));}
function scale(c,A){return A.map(v=>mul(c,v));}
function meq(A,B){return A.every((v,i)=>eq(v,B[i]));}
function determinant(A){return sub(mul(A[0],A[3]),mul(A[1],A[2]));}
const[a,b,c,d,e,t,m,n,v]=names.map((_,i)=>variable(i));
const I=matrix(one(),zero(),zero(),one());
const rv=matrix(zero(),one(),v,one()),sv=matrix(zero(),neg(one()),neg(v),zero());
const xv=ma(scale(a,I),scale(b,rv)),yv=matrix(c,d,mul(e,v,d),mul(e,c));
assert(meq(mm(rv,rv),ma(rv,scale(v,I))),"r quadratic");
assert(meq(mm(sv,sv),scale(v,I)),"s quadratic");
assert(meq(ma(rv,sv),matrix(zero(),zero(),zero(),one())),"pinning sum");
assert(meq(mm(xv,rv),mm(rv,xv)),"rho centralizer");
assert(eq(determinant(xv),sub(add(mul(a,a),mul(a,b)),mul(v,b,b))),"rho norm");
assert(meq(mm(yv,sv),scale(e,mm(sv,yv))),"signed sigma normalizer");
assert(eq(determinant(yv),mul(e,sub(mul(c,c),mul(v,d,d)))),"sigma norm");
const r=matrix(zero(),one(),neg(one()),one()),s=matrix(zero(),neg(one()),one(),zero());
const ri=matrix(one(),neg(one()),one(),zero()),si=matrix(zero(),one(),neg(one()),zero());
const mi=variable(6,-1),U=matrix(m,n,zero(),one()),Ui=matrix(mi,neg(mul(n,mi)),zero(),one());
assert(meq(mm(U,Ui),I),"Borel inverse");
const xi=mm(mm(mm(U,r),Ui),ri),eta=mm(mm(mm(U,s),Ui),si);
const expectedXi=matrix(add(m,n,mul(sub(mul(n,n),n),mi)),mul(n,mi),add(one(),mul(sub(n,one()),mi)),mi);
const expectedEta=matrix(add(m,mul(n,n,mi)),mul(n,mi),mul(n,mi),mi);
assert(meq(xi,expectedXi)&&meq(eta,expectedEta),"universal commutators");
assert(eq(determinant(xi),one())&&eq(determinant(eta),one()),"commutator determinants");
const arbitrary=matrix(a,b,c,d),complement=matrix(neg(a),neg(b),neg(c),sub(m,d));
assert(eq(sub(determinant(complement),determinant(arbitrary)),neg(mul(m,a))),"pinning determinant difference");
log("UNIVERSAL LAURENT-POLYNOMIAL ASSERTIONS PASSED over Z[a,b,c,d,e,t,m^+-1,n,v]/(e^2-1,t^2-1).");
function gcd(a,b){while(b)[a,b]=[b,a%b];return a;}
function mod(a,n){return((a%n)+n)%n;}
function matmul(A,B,n){return[mod(A[0]*B[0]+A[1]*B[2],n),mod(A[0]*B[1]+A[1]*B[3],n),mod(A[2]*B[0]+A[3]*B[2],n),mod(A[2]*B[1]+A[3]*B[3],n)];}
function det(A,n){return mod(A[0]*A[3]-A[1]*A[2],n);}
function invint(a,n){for(let x=0;x<n;x++)if(mod(a*x,n)===1)return x;throw new Error("not unit");}
function inverse(A,n){const q=invint(det(A,n),n);return[q*A[3],-q*A[1],-q*A[2],q*A[0]].map(z=>mod(z,n));}
function same(A,B){return A.every((z,i)=>z===B[i]);}
function finite(n){
 const units=Array.from({length:n},(_,i)=>i).filter(x=>gcd(x,n)===1),roots=units.filter(x=>mod(x*x,n)===1);
 const nr=Array(n).fill(0),ns=Array(n).fill(0),Tr=[],Ns=[],SL=[],GL=[],R=[],S=[];
 for(let a=0;a<n;a++)for(let b=0;b<n;b++){
  const q=mod(a*a+a*b+b*b,n);if(gcd(q,n)===1){nr[q]++;Tr.push([a,b,mod(-b,n),mod(a+b,n)]);}
  const z=mod(a*a+b*b,n);if(gcd(z,n)===1){ns[z]++;for(const e of roots)Ns.push([a,b,mod(-e*b,n),mod(e*a,n)]);}
 }
 for(let a=0;a<n;a++)for(let b=0;b<n;b++)for(let c=0;c<n;c++)for(let d=0;d<n;d++){
  const A=[a,b,c,d],q=det(A,n);if(gcd(q,n)===1)GL.push(A);
  if(q===1){SL.push(A);if(mod(a+d,n)===1)R.push(A);if(mod(a+d,n)===0)S.push(A);}
 }
 let H=0,H00=0;
 for(const q of units){H00+=ns[q]*nr[q];for(const e of roots)for(const t of roots)H+=ns[q]*nr[mod(e*t*q,n)];}
 let triples=0;
 for(const r of R)for(const s of S)if(det(r.map((x,i)=>mod(x+s[i],n)),n)===0)triples++;
 assert(triples===SL.length,"triple count "+n);
 const squares=new Set(units.map(x=>mod(x*x,n))),orbitCount=units.length/squares.size,orbitSize=SL.length/roots.length;
 assert(orbitCount*orbitSize===triples,"orbit partition "+n);
 let signed="not-enumerated";
 if([2,3,4].includes(n)){
  const images=new Set();
  for(const m of units)for(let h=0;h<n;h++){const U=[m,h,0,1];for(const x of Tr)for(const y of Ns)images.add(matmul(U,inverse(x,n),n).join(",")+"|"+matmul(U,inverse(y,n),n).join(","));}
  assert(images.size===units.length*n*Tr.length*Ns.length,"signed parametrization injective "+n);
  signed=images.size;
 }
 let finer=0;
 for(let i=0;i<2;i++)for(let j=0;j<2;j++)for(let w=0;w<n;w++){
  const multiplier=1-12*w+6*i+4*j;if(gcd(mod(multiplier,n),n)!==1)continue;
  for(let c=0;c<n;c++)for(let d=0;d<n;d++){
   const b=mod(i+j-2*w,n),h=mod(i+j-3*w,n),a=mod(c-d-i-j+2*w,n);
   assert(mod(a+b-c+d,n)===0&&mod(3*b-2*h-i-j,n)===0,"finer chart equations");
   assert(mod(4*h+2*i+1-multiplier,n)===0,"finer multiplier");finer++;
  }
 }
 log([n,H,SL.length,H*SL.length,H00*SL.length,triples,orbitCount,orbitSize,signed,finer].join("\t"));
}
log("n\tH\tSL2\tM\tM00\ttriples\tSLorbits\torbit_size\tsigned_image\tfiner_scheme");
for(const n of[2,3,5,7,11,13,4,8,9])finite(n);
const f5=[0,1,1,2];assert(det(f5,5)===4&&mod(f5[2]**2+f5[3]**2,5)===0,"weak pair counterexample");
log("F5 weak-pair witness: det(alpha)=4; lower-row Gaussian norm=0.");
const g=[1,0,1,1],g3=g.map(x=>mod(3*x,4));
assert(det(g,4)===1&&det(g3,4)===1&&mod(g[0]+g[3],4)===2&&mod(g3[0]+g3[3],4)===2&&!same(g,g3),"Z4 lift ambiguity");
log("Z/4 lift ambiguity: [[1,0],[1,1]] and [[3,0],[3,3]], determinant1 and trace2.");
log("ALL RECONSTRUCTED A/B ASSERTIONS PASSED.");
const rendered=output.join("\n")+"\n";
if(typeof text==="function")text(rendered);else console.log(rendered);
if(typeof store==="function")store("ab_reconstructed_output",rendered);
